None

Proof.

The proof mainly consists in defining the "new"
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
as the "complement" of
\mathrm{Z}
as defined in
Proposition

4.15
where
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
is replaced by its definition in
Table

19
. For example, for filter queries, since
\mathrm{Z}=(S_{D}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{Y}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{X}
, we obtain
\mathrm{Z}=S_{D}-(\mathrm{X}-\mathrm{Y})
and its complement
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=S_{r}-\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{X}-\mathrm{Y}
. For aggregation queries,
\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{X}_{1}
and
\mathrm{X}_{1}=\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}
and we obtain
\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{Y}-(\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X})
and its complement
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{Y}\cap\mathrm{X}
.
∎

We make the following observations on the rules of
Table

27
. First, the rule for Project is unchanged with respect to
Table

19
.
Second, when
Q
is an aggregate query
\mathpzc{Q}\,=\mathpzc{Agg}_{T}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{B}})\mid\mathrm{Y})
, the aggregable property for attribute
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{B}})
is computed using the rule of
Table

19
to guarantee the summarizability of attribute
B
. For attributes of
\mathrm{Y}
, the only refinement to the rule in
Table

19
is to restrict the scope of
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
. The same observation applies to the refined propagation rule for a pivot query.

Proposition 0 (aggregable properties with G-summarizability for binary queries).

Let
T(S)
and
T^{\prime}(S^{\prime})
be two analytic tables with dimension attributes
S_{D}\subseteq S
and
S^{\prime}_{D}\subseteq S^{\prime}
respectively,
S_{D}^{top}p
denote the highest attributes in
S_{D}
and
\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}
denote the highest attributes in
\mathrm{Y}=S_{D}\cap S^{\prime}_{D}
.
Let
T_{r}(S_{r})
be the result of a binary query between
T
and
T^{\prime}
and

\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}^{\prime}\in S_{r}\cap S
be an attribute of
T
with aggregable property
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}},\mathrm{X})
holding in
T
.
Then, for all queries
\mathpzc{Q}\,
satisfying the conditions of
Section

4.4
, the aggregable property
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}},\mathrm{X}^{\prime})
holds in
T_{r}
and is such that for all
\mathrm{Z}
where
\textsc{S}_{D}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{Z}
,
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}^{\prime}
is G-summarizable in
T
with respect to query
\mathpzc{Q}\,
, grouping set
\mathrm{Z}
, and function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
.

Proof.

As for unary queries, the proof mainly consists in defining the "new"
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
as
the "complement" of
\mathrm{Z}
as defined in
Proposition

4.15
where
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
is replaced by its definition in
Table

19
.
For dimension attributes
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}^{\prime}
, we can show that we always obtain a new
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
which is equal to the old
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
defined in
Table

19
.
For example, for right-merge and full-merge queries, if
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}^{\prime}
is a dimension attribute in
S_{D}
,
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{X}\cup S^{\prime}_{D}-\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{f}
and in the first case where
\mathrm{Z}=(S_{D}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}
, we obtain
\mathrm{Z}=S_{D}-(\mathrm{X}\cup S^{\prime}_{D}-\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{f})\cup\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}=S_{D}-(\mathrm{X}\cup S^{\prime}_{D}-\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{f}-\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top})=S_{D}-(\mathrm{X}\cup S^{\prime}_{D}-\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{f})
which we also obtain in the second case:
\mathrm{Z}=(S_{D}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{Y}=S_{D}-(\mathrm{X}\cup S^{\prime}_{D}-\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{f}-\mathrm{Y})=S_{D}-(\mathrm{X}\cup S^{\prime}_{D}-\mathrm{Y}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime}_{f})
.

For measure attributes,
\mathrm{Z}=\textsc{S}_{D}-\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{X}
, we obtain the new
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{X}
.
∎

We make the following observations on the rules of
Section

4.4
and
Section

4.5
.
First, all rules refine the conditions and actions of the propagation rules of
Table

21
by taking into account the restrictions described in
Proposition

4.15
.
Second, in the case of a right-merge, full-merge, union and difference query,
it is possible to search for any subset
\mathrm{Y}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{Y}
instead of
\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}
for which the conditions on
\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}
hold. If no such
\mathrm{Y}^{\prime}
is found then the set of attributes
\mathrm{Y}
must be removed from
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}
(we illustrated that in
Example

4.14
).
Third, note that the propagation rule for right-merge assumes that
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}^{\prime}
is an exclusive attribute of
T
. If
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}^{\prime}
is also in
T^{\prime}
then its aggregable property is computed using the propagation rule for left-merge.

4.5.
Wrapping up results on summarizability

To wrap up our results on summarizability and G-summarizability, we illustrate them using the motivating example presented in the introduction of this paper. We then discuss some directions for future work around the generation of explanations associated with the result of an analytic query.

Example 0.

Consider the example of interactive data analysis session of
Figure

2
on the tables in
Table

2
. Table
\mathrm{{T3}}
is obtained by a filter query
\mathpzc{Filter}_{\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}}(P|\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY,YEAR}})
and attribute

AMOUNT

in
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
is aggregable along all dimension attributes except attribute

YEAR

. By the G-summarizability rule for filter queries for attribute

AMOUNT

in
\mathrm{{T3}}
, we obtain the aggregable property
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{AMOUNT}}}(\mathrm{SUM},\mathrm{X}_{3})
where
\mathrm{X}_{3}
now contains all attributes of
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
except

COUNTRY

and

YEAR

. Since
the table
\mathrm{{T4}}
is obtained by summarizing

AMOUNT

by
\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CITY, STATE, COUNTRY, YEAR}}
, the aggregate operation over
\mathrm{{T3}}
leading to
\mathrm{{T4}}
is
correct
with respect to the G-summarizability of

AMOUNT

.

Next, the table
\mathrm{{T5}}
is obtained by a merge query adding the attribute

POP

from table
{DEM}
to table
\mathrm{{T4}}
. For attribute

POP

in
\mathrm{{T5}}
, we have
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{POP}}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}},\mathrm{X}_{5})
, where
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
and
\mathrm{X}_{5}
are defined according to
Section

4.4
for right merge (
{DEM}
is the "outer" merge table). We have join attributes
\mathrm{Y}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CITY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{STATE}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}}\}
with
\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}}\}
. However,
\pi_{\mathrm{Y}}(\sigma_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}}=^{\prime}USA^{\prime}\wedge\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}}=2018}(\mathrm{{T4}}))\subset\pi_{Y}(\sigma_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}}=^{\prime}USA^{\prime}\wedge\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}}=2018}(\mathrm{{DEM}}))
(city of ’Palo Alto’ is missing in
\mathrm{{T4}}
).
Thus, we have
\mathrm{X}_{5}=\mathrm{Y}
in the above aggregable property.
Consequently, the aggregation of

SUM(POP)

along

CITY

is
incorrect
with respect to the G-summarizability property.

Table
{DEM^{\prime}}
in
Table

6(a)
is obtained by aggregating the population in table
{DEM}
along attribute

CITY

. In table
{DEM}
, we have
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{POP}}}(\mathrm{SUM},\mathrm{X})
with
\mathrm{X}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CITY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{STATE}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}}\}
. So the aggregation along

CITY

leading to table
{DEM^{\prime}}
is
correct
, and by
Proposition

4.9
,
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{POP}}}(\mathrm{SUM},\mathrm{X}^{\prime})
holds in
{DEM^{\prime}}
with
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{X}\cap\mathrm{Y}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{STATE}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}}\}
(function
\mathrm{SUM}
is distributive). Next, in the merge result of
\mathrm{{T4}}
with
\mathrm{{DEM^{\prime}}}
, we have
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{POP}}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}},\mathrm{X}_{4})
, where
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
and
\mathrm{X}_{4}
are defined by the rule for outer merge (
{DEM^{\prime}}
is the outer merge table). We have
\mathrm{Y}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{STATE}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}}\}
and
\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}}\}
. We also have
\pi_{Y}(\mathrm{{DEM}}\ltimes_{\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}}\mathrm{{T4}})=\pi_{Y}(\mathrm{{DEM}})
, and since
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{POP}}}(\mathrm{SUM},\mathrm{X}^{\prime})
holds in
\mathrm{{DEM^{\prime}}}
, we get
\mathrm{X}_{4}=X^{\prime}-\mathrm{\mathrm{Y}}^{top}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{STATE}}\}
. Finally, since
\mathrm{Y}
does not literally determine the schema of
\mathrm{{T4}}
,
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
must be restricted to one of
\{\mathrm{COUNT\_DISTINCT},\mathrm{MIN},\mathrm{MAX}\}
.

The question that naturally arises is what options should be provided to the end user when G-summarizability is violated (i.e., the conditions given by aggregable properties are not satisfied).
A first option is to reject an incorrect aggregate query with respect to G-summarizability and return the grouping set of the aggregable property as an explanation. This is the option we have described in this paper. However, another option could be to accept the aggregate query provided that some metadata is added to the resulting table to enable a non-ambiguous and correct interpretation of the tuples in that table.
We explain the idea in the next example and leave it for future work.

Example 0.

Consider again the interactive session of
Figure

2
and suppose that the first filter query on
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
is:
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{STATE}}\neq null
(expecting that this eliminates all European countries) and

YEAR

= ’2018’.
Next, suppose that the aggregate operation over
\mathrm{{T3}}
sums

AMOUNT

for each partition defined by attributes

COUNTRY

and

YEAR

. It will be difficult for an end user to figure out that an incorrect aggregate value has been computed for country ’USA’ if the user ignores that ’Washington DC’ has no state. With our current proposition, this aggregate operation will be rejected since the grouping set does not include attribute

STATE

(we would have
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{AMOUNT}}}(\mathrm{SUM},\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CITY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}}\})
in
\mathrm{{T3}}
). However, to disambiguate the result of the aggregate operation, it would be sufficient to "attach" the filter condition
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{STATE}}\neq null
as metadata to table
\mathrm{{T4}}
to indicate that the amount for stores in those states has not been accounted for. It is then possible for the end user to query those stores from table
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
to visualize them and decide if table
\mathrm{{T4}}
is satisfactory.

The same principle applies to the result of the left-merge operation between
\mathrm{{T4}}
and
\mathrm{{DEM}}
. As we have seen in the previous example, we have
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{POP}}}(\mathrm{SUM},\mathrm{X}_{5})
in
\mathrm{{T5}}
and the aggregate operation on
\mathrm{{T5}}
is rejected. However, we could accept the operation and simply mark that measure attribute

SUM(POP)

now depends on dimension
{SALESORG}
. This would indicate that the population is summed for the cities in
{SALESORG}
, that is, the cities that have stores.

The idea is therefore to make each analytic table, resulting from an interactive data analysis session, "self-explanatory" with respect to its aggregated attributes.

5.
Related Work

In this section, we focus on previous works that propose conditions on the schema of a fact table, or on the parameters of an aggregate query expressed over that fact table, to determine if the aggregate query returns a correct result with respect to some summarizability definition.
Previous papers on summarizability use heterogeneous notations and concepts and are sometimes difficult to read because they lack some details or hide some assumptions. To facilitate comparisons with our work, we reformulated each previous proposition using the notations introduced in this paper.

In our detailed analysis, we establish the following:

(1)

Our data model is more general than the data models considered by previous work.

(2)

In the case of a sequence of two aggregate queries,
Q_{1}
followed by
Q_{2}
, our sufficient conditions to determine if
Q_{2}
is correct subsume the conditions proposed by previous work.

(3)

To our best knowledge, no previous work addressed the case of a sequence made of an arbitrary analytic query followed by an aggregate query, which is addressed by our notion of G-summarizability.

5.1.
Summarizability of a query over a statistical object

The notion of
summarizability
was initially defined by Rafanelli and Shoshani
(
Rafanelli and
Shoshani 1990
)
for statistical databases and later refined in their seminal paper
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
. In their context,
base data
, also referred to as "micro-data", describe all the details about the objects or individuals over which a summarization operation can be applied to produce a so-called
statistical object
, also referred to as "macro-data".
There are a few constraints. In the base data, an
object of interest
must be identified (e.g., a product, a customer, a store) using some attributes, all other attributes being viewed as "descriptors" of the object. A statistical object is a table defined by a summarized attribute (i.e., an attribute of the base data on which a summarization function is applied) and a set of "category" attributes defining the partitions of the base table on which the summarization function is applied.
Using the terminology defined in
Section

2
, base data can be modelled as a non-analytic table and summarization operations are aggregate queries which ignore partitions with null values in their partition identifiers. We shall keep the expression "summarization operation" to distinguish it from our analytic aggregate operation that handles null values as regular values. A statistical object can be modeled as a fact table that results from a summarization operation over the base data where category attributes represent dimensions and the summarized attribute is a measure.

The fact tables that represent statistical objects in
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
are however more restricted than the fact tables enabled by our data model.
Firstly, dimensions are restricted to
strict
hierarchies, that is, each attribute has at most one parent attribute in the hierarchy type, each attribute value of a dimension has at most one parent attribute value in the hierarchy of the dimension, and hierarchy types must have a single bottom and top level attribute.
Secondly, all dimensions in a fact table must be independent (that is, no attribute in some dimension functionally depends on an attribute of another dimension).
Finally, all facts in a fact table have the same dimensions, i.e., the measure attribute does not depend on a subset of the dimensions.

An important distinction, with respect to multidimensional data models, is that there is no notion of managed dimensions in
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
;
Rafanelli and
Shoshani 1990
)
. The notion of dimension hierarchy is purely local to a statistical object and depends on the functional dependencies that are supposed to hold in the base data on which the object is built. If these dependencies change, the dimensions hierarchies are adjusted to fit the strictness constraint explained before.

In
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
;
Rafanelli and
Shoshani 1990
)
, summarizability is defined as the property of a summarized attribute
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})
of a fact table (statistical object)
T
which guarantees that a summarization operation
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}
over
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})
produces a
correct
result.
Suppose we have a base table
T_{0}
and a fact table
T(S)
, which results from a summarization operation applying aggregation function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
on attribute

A

along attributes
\mathrm{X}
:

T=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T_{0}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(A)\mid\mathrm{X})

where
\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}
denotes a summarization operation that does not consider partitions where an attribute of
\mathrm{X}
has a null value. Let
\mathpzc{Q}\,
be a summarization query over
T
:

\mathpzc{Q}\,=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}))|\mathrm{Z})

where
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}
is a function applicable to the summarized attribute
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})
of
T
, and
\mathrm{Z}
is a set of dimension attributes in
\mathrm{X}
.
Then, the summarization query
\mathpzc{Q}\,
is said to be
correct
, if the following condition holds:

\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T_{0}}(F(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})|\mathrm{Z})=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(G(F(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}))|\mathrm{Z})

To guarantee the correctness of summarization query
\mathpzc{Q}\,
,
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
defines three necessary properties on the summarization query
\mathpzc{Q}\,
and the dimensions
{D}
of
T_{0}
. Let
\mathrm{X}_{D}\subseteq\mathrm{X}
be the set of dimension attributes for dimension
{D}
in
T
,
\mathrm{X}_{D}^{bot}\in X_{D}
be the bottom level attribute of
{D}
in
T
and
D^{bot}
be the bottom level attribute of
{D}
in
T_{0}
(then,
D^{bot}=X_{D}^{bot}
or
D^{bot}\preccurlyeq^{*}X_{D}^{bot}
).
The properties are:

(1)

Disjointness
. For each dimension
{D}
along which summarization is done in
\mathpzc{Q}\,
, at least one of the following conditions must hold:
(a)
\mathrm{X}_{D}-Z=\{D^{bot}\}
consists of the bottom level attribute of
{D}
and the partitions of
T_{0}
using
D^{bot}
are disjoint with respect to the identifier attributes of the object of interest in
T_{0}
;
(b) every value in
T_{0}
of a dimension attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{1}
that is a below an attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{2}
in
\mathrm{X}_{D}-Z
in
{D}
(
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{1}\preccurlyeq^{*}\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{2}
) must map to a single value of its parent attribute (many-to-one mapping);

(2)

Completeness with respect to
F(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})
. For each dimension
{D}
of
T
, the domain of each attribute in
\mathrm{X}_{D}
is
complete
in
T_{0}
, if both of the following conditions hold:
(a)
all values of the identifier attributes of the object of interest which are required by
F(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})
appear in
T_{0}
,
and
(b) one of the two following conditions holds:
if
\mathrm{X}_{D}-Z=\{D_{bot}\}
consists of the bottom level attribute of
{D}
in
T_{0}
then the value of this attribute cannot be
null
in
T_{0}
. Otherwise,
every value of a dimension attribute that is a child of
\mathrm{X}_{D}^{bot}
must map to a parent value in
\mathrm{X}_{D}^{bot}
within
T_{0}
.

(3)

Applicable summary function
. The summary function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}
is "applicable" to the summarized attribute
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(A)
with respect to all dimensions along which summarization is done in
\mathpzc{Q}\,
.

We now analyze each one of the previous conditions and relate it to our work.

Disjointness:

In the original formulation of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
, the disjointness property requires that the dimension attributes along which summarization is done form disjoint subsets over the "objects of interest" defined in the base table. Two different disjointness conditions are then given, depending on whether the dimension attribute is a bottom attribute of the dimension or not.
The goal of the disjointness property is mainly to avoid double counting by overlapping subsets.
In our work, we address the disjointness property by defining aggregable properties and propagation rules. Each aggregable property describes for a given attribute

A

in fact table
T_{0}
along which attributes it can be aggregated using some function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
. The summarizability preserving propagation rules then produce all aggregable properties of
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(A)
in
T
after the aggregation operation on
T_{0}
is done (see
Proposition

3.5
).
Summarizability is defined using the notion of function distributivity and literal functional dependencies.
There is no need to choose an object of interest in the base data for defining the scope of summarizability, and any attribute can be summarized.

Completeness:

In completeness condition (2.a) of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
, it is not clear how the set of "all possible values" for the identifier attributes is determined to assess completeness. Thus, we used the interpretation that the possible values are those listed in some reference directory. Furthermore, condition (2.a) applies to the identifier values required for computing
F(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})
which means that the user who is formulating query
\mathpzc{Q}\,
must decide whether completeness is needed or whether the values listed in
T
are sufficient to compute a summary attribute. We shall see in
Example

5.2
that this condition (2.a) is useless for checking summarizability.
The Item (2.b) of the completeness condition
is required because summarization operations cannot deal with attributes of
Z
that have null values. In our work, we do not have such a restriction since our SQL aggregate operations handle null values as regular values.

Function applicability:

The third condition focuses on testing the compatibility between the type of dimensions and the type of measures used in
T
. The following types of measures can be used by the designer of a fact table:
stock
(i.e., a simple value at a particular point in time),
flow
(i.e., cumulative values over a period of time) and
value-per-unit
(i.e, determined value for a fixed time). Dimensions can be of type temporal or non-temporal. When a measure attribute

A

is aggregated using a given function over some dimension
{D}
, the types of

A

and
{D}
should be compatible with respect to that function.
In our work, applicable functions are captured by the more general notion of aggregable property, which leaves the method to decide which function is applicable to an attribute open. Furthermore, we use propagation rules and default rules to infer the functions that are applicable to an attribute that has been aggregated. Thus, a user is not forced to define the type of the measure attributes in
T
since aggregable properties for these attributes will be automatically computed using propagation rule (see
Proposition

3.5
).

The following two examples illustrate the conditions of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)

and emphasize the differences with our work.

.

Example 0.

Consider the base table
\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_LIST}}
(

PROD_SKU

,

COUNTRY

,

BRAND

,

YEAR

,

QTY

), whose instance is displayed on
Table

28
, and where the "object of interest" is a product identified by

PROD_SKU

.
To comply with the constraint of strict dimensions defined by
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
, attribute

PROD_SKU

must belong to a separate dimension (it determines no other attribute), attributes

BRAND

,

COUNTRY

belong to a dimension
{MKT\_PROD}
, and attribute

YEAR

belongs to a dimension
{TIME}
.

Next, suppose that we define a statistical object

\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_SUM}}
(

BRAND

,

YEAR

,

NB_PROD_SKU

) built using a summarization query with function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}=\mathrm{COUNT\_DISTINCT}
:

\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_SUM}}=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{PRODUCT\_LIST}(\mathrm{COUNT\_DISTINCT}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}})|\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{BRAND}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}})

The result is displayed on
Table

29
(we renamed the summarized attribute as

NB_PROD_SKU

). The user should associate the summarized attribute

NB_PROD_SKU

with a type
flow
since the number of distinct products sold is cumulative over time.

Table 29
.

\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_SUM}}

The first summarization query
\mathpzc{Q}\,_{1}
aggregates

NB_PROD_SKU

along

YEAR

using function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}=\mathrm{SUM}
to count the number of distinct products by brand :

Q_{1}=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{PRODUCT\_SUM}(\mathrm{SUM}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{NB\_PROD\_SKU}})|\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{BRAND}})

Since

YEAR

is a bottom attribute of dimension
{D}={TIME}
in
T_{0}
, Item (1.a) of the disjointness condition must be tested over
T_{0}
. It fails because the product
“cz-tshirt-s”
belongs to two different partitions of
T_{0}=\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_LIST}}
by

YEAR

.
Thus, query
Q_{1}
is incorrect. Indeed, the number of distinct products by brand reported by query
Q_{1}
(e.g., value
2
for brand Zora) would be different from the number directly computed from
\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_LIST}}
(e.g., 1 for brand Zora).

The second summarization query
\mathpzc{Q}\,_{2}
aggregates

NB_PROD_SKU

along

BRAND

:

Q_{2}=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{PRODUCT\_SUM}(\mathrm{SUM}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{NB\_PROD\_SKU}})|\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}})

Since

BRAND

is again a bottom attribute of dimension
{MKT\_PROD}
in
T_{0}
, Item (1.a) of the disjointness condition must be tested. It fails since the product
“cz-tshirt-s”
maps to different partitions of
T_{0}
by

BRAND

. Thus, query
Q_{2}
is also incorrect.
Again, the number of distinct products by year reported by query
Q_{2}
(e.g., value 2 for year 2018) would be different from the number directly computed from
\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_LIST}}
(e.g., 1 for year 2018).

By comparison with our work,
let’s assume that the aggregable property
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}}}(\mathrm{COUNT\_DISTINCT}\mid\mathrm{Z})
, where
\mathrm{Z}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{BRAND}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}}\}
, has been validated by the designer of table
\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_LIST}}
. Then, by
Proposition

4.9
, we infer that the aggregable property controlling summarizability,
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{NB\_PROD\_SKU}}}(\mathrm{SUM}\mid\emptyset)
, holds in
\mathrm{{PRODUCT\_SUM}}
. Therefore, we also detect that both
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are incorrect. However, our detection does not require running any query over the base data, unlike the disjointness condition of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
. We only use the knowledge of the attribute graphs of the dimensions and of the aggregable properties defined on fact tables.

Example 0.

Suppose that we have a base object, represented by table
\mathrm{STORE\_SALES}
introduced earlier (see
Table

8(d)
), in which stores are the objects of interest (identified by

STORE_ID

). A statistical object, modeled by fact table
\mathrm{STORE\_SALES\_YEARLY}
(displayed in
Table

30(a)
), is computed using the following summarization query:

\mathrm{STORE\_SALES\_YEARLY}=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{STORE\_SALES}(\mathrm{SUM}(AMOUNT)|\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CITY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{STATE}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}})

We assume that the summarized attribute

SUM(AMOUNT)

is renamed as

AMOUNT

and has been associated with type
flow
, i.e., it can be summed along any dimension.
To fulfill the constraints on dimensions, there will be four dimension hierarchies respectively formed of the following attributes: {

CITY

}, {

STATE

}, {

COUNTRY

}, and {

YEAR

}.

Consider the following summarization query
\mathpzc{Q}\,_{3}
whose result is displayed in

:

Q_{3}=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{STORE\_SALES}(\mathrm{SUM}(AMOUNT)|\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{COUNTRY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{YEAR}})

The disjointness condition is obviously satisfied since a store in table
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
belongs to a single partition by

CITY

and a single partition by

STATE

.
Now, assume that the directory of all possible values for

STORE_ID

is given by the dimension table
{SALESORG}
of
Table

8(d)
. Then, the completeness condition (2.a) is violated
since store
Ca\_02
is missing in the list of values of

STORE_ID

in
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
. However, from a strict summarizability point of view, it is easy to see that the result of
Q_{3}
is the same as the result of the same aggregation executed over table
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
. So completeness constraint (2.a) is useless for checking summarizability.

Completeness constraint (2.b) is also violated since bottom attribute

STATE

,
has a
null
value in
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
for city
‘Paris’
.
Hence, there is no tuple for
‘Paris’
in
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES\_YEARLY}}
and therefore also no tuple for country
‘France’
in the result of
Q_{3}
. The summarizability property is thus clearly violated since the result of
Q_{3}
applied to
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
returns a different result containing the tuple
(France,2017,45.1)
.
In our work,
\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES\_YEARLY}}
would contain a tuple for city
‘Paris’
with a null value for

STATE

, and therefore a tuple for country
‘France’
in the result of
Q_{3}
.

5.2.
Summarizability of attributes in fact tables

In previous works on multidimensional databases, summarizability is expressed over fact tables in a way similar to our
Definition

4.1
illustrated by
Figure

9
. In this context, we first review the work of
(
Pedersen
et al. 1999
;
Pedersen
et al. 2001
)
that uses a multidimensional data model close to ours, and generalizes the summarizability conditions of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
.
Note that other solutions propose alternative analytic data models and methods to modify the representation of dimensions to enforce summarizability. In the following, we do not consider these solutions for which a survey can be found in
(
Mazón et al. 2009
)
.
Thus, like in our work, we concentrate on summarizability models which characterize correct compositions of two aggregation queries over fact tables with fixed dimensions.

Given a summarization query
\mathpzc{Q}\,=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(F(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})|\mathrm{X})
over a fact table
T(\textsc{S})
, we define
summarizability
as a property of an attribute

A

with respect to grouping set
\mathrm{X}
and function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
. More exactly,
(
Pedersen
et al. 2001
)
considers an attribute

A

to be summarizable with respect to
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
if for any subset
Z\subset\mathrm{X}
of dimension attributes, the following condition holds:

(4)

\displaystyle\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})|\mathrm{Z})=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{\mathpzc{Q}\,}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}))|\mathrm{Z})

Note that this
definition is more restrictive than our notion of summarizability in
Definition

4.1
, since it imposes that the same function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
is used in the two queries.
The following conditions provided by
(
Pedersen
et al. 2001
)
are
sufficient for ensuring
Equation

4
. Let
\mathrm{X}_{D}
denote the set of dimension attributes for dimension
{D}
in
\mathrm{X}
:

(1)

Function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
is
applicable
to

A

,

(2)

Function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
is
distributive
over the domain of values in

A

.

(3)

For every dimension
{D}
, all the value mappings between the bottom level attribute of
{D}
in
T
and any attribute of
\mathrm{X}_{D}
are many to one.

(4)

For every dimension
{D}
, every value of an attribute in
T
that is a sub-level of an attribute of
\mathrm{X}_{D}
must be non null.

We now comment each condition and draw comparisons with
our work.
In the first condition,
(
Pedersen
et al. 2001
)
assumes that we know the functions that are applicable to

A

. Three types of aggregation functions are distinguished: (1) functions, which are applicable to data that can be added together (e.g.,
\mathrm{SUM}
,
\mathrm{COUNT}
,
\mathrm{AVG}
), (2) functions, which are applicable to data that can be used for average calculations (e.g.,
\mathrm{COUNT}
,
\mathrm{AVG}
,
\mathrm{MIN}
,
\mathrm{MAX}
), and (3) functions which are applicable to data that can only be counted.
However, unlike our work, the notion of aggregation type does not consider the dimensions along which a summarization can be performed.
The second condition uses a definition of distributive function that is more restrictive than our
Definition

4.2
because it requires that
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
is such that for any two sets,
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
,
F(V_{1},\cup V_{2})=F(F(V_{1})\cup F(V_{2}))
. Thus, functions like
\mathrm{COUNT}
are discarded.
The third condition is similar to the disjointness condition of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
which we already compared with our work.
The fourth condition is equivalent to the second completeness condition (2.b) of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
but does not cover the first completeness condition (2.a). As mentioned before, aggregation functions in our work handle null values in dimensions as regular values, and can ignore condition (2.b) to guarantee summarizability.

5.3.
Multidimensional normal forms

Several works proposed multidimensional normal forms for analytic tables that provide guarantees for the correctness of summarization queries
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
. These normal forms can be used to design dimension and fact tables over which correct summarization queries can be easily detected and evaluated. In the sequel, we are not interested in the design aspects but we examine the definitions of these normal forms as a way to formulate summarizability conditions.

We first introduce a few concepts and vocabulary.
A dimension attribute (also called a dimension level) which can have a
null
value is called
optional
and otherwise called
mandatory
. Dimensions must have a single bottom level type and an implicit top level type, called
ALL
.
The hierarchy in each dimension is strict using "functional dependencies with nulls" (NFD), that is, each attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
has at most one parent attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
and each arc
(A_{i},A_{j})
in the attribute graph of the dimension is labelled with a
\mathbf{1}
(
Section

2.2
).

The novelty of
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
in comparison with
(
Pedersen
et al. 2001
)
is the following. A dimension is also associated with a (possibly empty) set of
context dependencies
: let
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
and
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
be two dimension attributes of a dimension
{D}
such that
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
is optional,
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}\neq ALL
, and
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}\preccurlyeq A_{j}
. If
c\in dom(A_{j})
and
c\neq null
, then
(A_{i},A_{j},c)
is a context dependency for
{D}
stating that
for every tuple
T
of the dimension table,
t.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}=c\Leftrightarrow t.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}\neq null
. Intuitively, the interpretation of a context dependency is that
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
plays the role of a discriminating attribute in the hierarchy and value
c
is the discriminating value to indicate when the optional attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
has a non-null value. Note that the use of an equivalence (
\Leftrightarrow
), in the above formula, is quite strong since it forces the existence of a
single
discriminating value.

In
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
, a fact table is defined over a set of dimensions at the finest level of detail, that is, all dimension attributes of a dimension are included in the schema of the fact table, and each measure in the fact table is determined (with NFD constraints) by the set of bottom level dimension attributes in each dimension. Thus, fact tables are similar to the "micro data" of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
.
Note that although fact tables in
(
Pedersen
et al. 2001
)
can represent facts at a coarser granularity, their summarizability conditions impose that the bottom level dimension attributes of each dimension determine (with NFD constraints) all measure attributes.
In
(
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
,
summarizability constraints
express the dimension hierarchy along which a measure can be aggregated using some aggregation function. However, no formal treatment of summarizability constraints is provided, unlike our use of aggregable properties for analyzing aggregate queries and propagating summarizability constraints to query results. In
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
)
, the same categorization of measure attributes as
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
is used.

In
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
)
, two different multidimensional normal forms are presented. The first one is called
Multidimensional Normal Form (MNF)
and provides conditions similar to
the work of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
and
(
Pedersen
et al. 2001
)
. The second one, called
Generalized Multidimensional Normal Form (GMNF)
, provides more general conditions which have been slightly extended in
(
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
.

In the following we will describe the GMNF as defined by
(
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
. Let
T
be a fact table defined over a set of dimensions with a measure (summary) attribute

A

. Then
T
is in GMNF if all of the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

For each dimension
{D}
of
T
:

(a)

for every optional dimension attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
of
{D}
, there exists a context dependency
(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j},c)
in
{D}
;

(b)

the values of the bottom level dimension attribute in
T
are complete.

(2)

All dimensions are mutually independent, i.e., there exists no NFD between any two dimension attributes of two distinct dimensions.

(3)

The set of (unique) bottom level attributes of all dimensions functionally determines (FD) attribute

A

.

We comment these conditions. Condition
1a
and condition
3
enforce the third and fourth conditions of
(
Pedersen
et al. 2001
)
presented in
Section

5.2
. Indeed, if all dimension attributes are mandatory, they cannot have null values and all dependencies between dimension attributes become functional dependencies. Condition
1b
is analogous to the completeness condition of
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
. Here again, the means to test this requirement are left unspecified and seem to require some external knowledge. Finally, condition
2
ensures that dimensions do not share dimension attributes and is not really needed for guaranteeing summarizability.
Note that by conditions
2
and
3
the bottom level dimension attributes in the schema of
T
form a primary key in
T
and there is no other primary key for

A

.
The novelty with respect to the previous models is brought by
1a
. It constrains the semantics of every optional dimension attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
so that there exists at an upper level an attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
that plays the role of discriminator for
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
. Note that
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
can itself be an optional attribute, in which case there will again be a context dependency
(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{k},c^{\prime})
in
{D}
. Eventually, the discriminator attribute will be a mandatory attribute since by definition of context dependency, the upper level attribute cannot be
ALL
.

We can now reformulate the previous GMNF conditions on dimensions as summarizability conditions on measure attributes and aggregate queries.
Let
T
be a fact table in GMNF, and
\mathpzc{Q}\,=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})|\mathrm{X})
be a summarization query over
T
. Then,

A

is
summarizable
with respect to function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
and grouping set
\mathrm{X}
,
i.e
.
, for all
\mathrm{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{X}
:
\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}})|\mathrm{Z})=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{\mathpzc{Q}\,}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}))|\mathrm{Z})
) if the following conditions hold:

(1)

\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
is applicable to

A

with respect to any subset of
\mathrm{X}
in the result of
\mathpzc{Q}\,
.

(2)

One of the two conditions hold:

(a)

\mathrm{X}
does not contain any optional dimension attribute, or

(b)

if
\mathrm{X}
contains an optional attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
then, assuming that
(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j},c)
is the associated context dependency, a filter condition:
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}=c
must be applied on
T
before the summarization query is applied

Here, the condition
1
is determined using either the categorization of

A

(
Lehner
et al. 1998
)
or the summarizability constraints on

A

(
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
, the latter being more precise as we stated earlier. The condition
2
states that summarizability holds provided that a (filtered) subset of the fact table is considered, and this subset is given by the context dependencies of the dimensions over which the fact table is defined.

Example 0.

Consider a product dimension,
{PROD\_NEW}
, whose attribute graph is depicted in
Figure

12
. A new optional attribute

VIDEO_RES

provides the video resolution for products with screens where
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}}\preccurlyeq\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}
and
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}}\preccurlyeq\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}}
. Consider the fact table
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}
over
{PROD\_NEW}
whose instance is displayed in
Table

31
.

Figure 12
.

{PROD\_NEW}

Assume that the designer of the dimension table
{PROD\_NEW}
defined a context dependency :
(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}},\text{\ltxml@iqmark@open\textquoteleft\penalty\hskip-0.0002pt\hskip 0.0002ptvideo\textquoteright\ltxml@iqmark@close\kern-0.0002pt\kern 0.0002pt{}})
. Then, table
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}
is in GMNF if we assume that all products are listed in the table. It is easy to see that conditions
2
and
3
are satisfied on the attribute graph. Condition
1
is also satisfied because the only optional attribute

VIDEO_RES

has an upper level discriminating attribute with value
‘video’
.

. Then

\mathrm{SUM}
(

,

) and Samsung (

).

We now compare GMNF with our work in the same context. Going back to
Example

5.3
, attribute

AMOUNT

is
literally
determined by the minimal subset of dimension attributes {

PROD_SKU

}, which also determines all other dimension attributes of
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}

\mathrm{SUM}

, by
Definition

3.1
on

3.1
,
\mathbf{agg}_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{AMOUNT}}}(\mathrm{SUM},Z)
holds in
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}
, where
Z
is the set of all dimension attributes of
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}
. Consider the query
Q_{1}
of
Example

5.3
. By

Proposition

4.4
, attribute

AMOUNT

is summarizable with respect to
\mathrm{SUM}
and
\mathrm{X}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{BRAND}}\}
since
\mathrm{X}\subset Z
and
\mathrm{SUM}
is distributive.
Now if
\mathrm{X}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{BRAND}}\}
, since
\mathrm{X}\subset Z
, attribute

AMOUNT

is also summarizable with respect to
\mathrm{SUM}
and
\mathrm{X}
, without requiring any pre-filtering of
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}
. The reason is our usage of SQL aggregation operations that considers null values as regular values.
Indeed, it is easy to see that the summarizability condition is satisfied by looking at the result of
\mathpzc{Q}\,
, displayed in

.

\mathrm{{STORE\_SALES}}
of

is not in GMNF since condition 1 is violated. The optional

STATE

has a null value for different countries and it is not possible to create a
single
context dependency for attribute

STATE

using either attribute

COUNTRY

or

. Thus, in our work, by accepting partition identifiers with null values, we handle cases of summarizability that are rejected by the conditions based on GMNF.

5.4.
Reasoning over constraints on dimensions

In
(
Hurtado
et al. 2005
)
, the summarizability constraints on dimensions generalize the idea of context dependencies introduced in
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
.
The multidimensional data model is restricted as follows. All dimension hierarchies have one top level attribute called

ALL

and possibly multiple bottom level attributes. As in
(
Pedersen
et al. 2001
;
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
, a dimension attribute can have multiple parent dimension attributes in the hierarchy (such dimensions are called "heterogeneous"), and there can be both, mandatory and optional dimension attributes. Every child-parent attribute mapping should be functional (i.e., every value only maps to one parent value). This is equivalent to the existence of an NFD dependency from any attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
to attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
where
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}\preccurlyeq A_{j}
. As in
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
, fact tables are defined over dimensions at the finest level of detail, that is, the schema of the fact table includes the bottom level attributes of the dimensions. Measure attributes are determined by
all
the dimensions and can only be aggregated using distributive functions (defined as in
Definition

4.2
). Dimensions are also supposed to be mutually independent in a fact table.
Summarizability is defined as a property of dimensions and any fact table built over summarizable dimensions has summarizable measures.
Let
{D}
be a dimension,
\mathrm{X}
be a subset of dimension attributes in
{D}
, and

B

a dimension attribute in
{D}
such that
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}\preccurlyeq\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{B}}
for some attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}\in\mathrm{X}
.
Attribute

B

is
summarizable from
\mathrm{X}
in
{D}
if and only if for every fact table
T
defined over
{D}
, every measure attribute

M

of
T
, every set
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{X}
,
and every distributive aggregate function
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
using
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}
that is applicable to
M
, we have:

(5)

\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(F(M)|\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{B}})=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T^{\prime}}(G(F(M))|\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{B}})\mathrm{\;where\;}T^{\prime}=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{M}})|\mathrm{X}^{\prime}\cup\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{B}})

The above definition of summarizability relates to summarizability conditions as follows. In
Definition

4.1
, we consider
a fact table
T^{\prime}=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{M}})\mid\mathrm{X})
resulting from a summarization query over a fact table
T
, where
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
is a distributive function using
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}
. Then we consider that

M

is summarizable with respect to
\mathrm{X}
and
\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}
when
\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{M}})|\mathrm{Z})=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T^{\prime}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{M}}))|\mathrm{Z})
for any
Z\subset X
. In this case, query
\mathpzc{Q}\,=\mathpzc{Agg}^{*}_{T^{\prime}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{G}}(\mathrm{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{F}}(M)|\mathrm{Z})
is considered to be correct by
(
Hurtado
et al. 2005
)
. In the above definition, Condition
5
must hold for any grouping set
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{X}
in the query defining
T^{\prime}
, but
\mathrm{Z}
is restricted to

B

. Therefore, the condition to determine if query
\mathpzc{Q}\,
is correct, is to enforce that
every attribute

B

of any dimension
{D}
in
\mathrm{Z}
is summarizable from
\mathrm{X}_{D}
, where
\mathrm{X}_{D}
is the set of dimension attributes of
{D}
in
\mathrm{X}

(
Equation

5
must hold for all attributes

B

of any dimension
{D}
and any subset of attributes
\mathrm{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{X}_{D}
).

(
Hurtado
et al. 2005
)
show that the summarizability Condition
5
can also be expressed independently of the fact tables which refer to a given dimension: attribute

B

is summarizable from
\mathrm{X}
in dimension
{D}
if and only if for every bottom level attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{\bot}
of
{D}
, the following equality holds, where
\pi
denotes the relational duplicate elimination projection and
\bowtie
denotes the null-eliminating join:

(6)

\pi_{A_{\bot},B}(D)=\bigcup_{A_{i}\in X}(\pi_{A_{\bot},A_{i}}(D)\bowtie\pi_{A_{i},B}(D))

Example 0.

Consider the product dimension
{PROD\_NEW}
in
Table

33
. Using
Equation

6
, we can show that attribute

CATEGORY

is summarizable from
\mathrm{X}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}\}
because

\pi_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}}({PROD\_NEW})
is equal to the join
\pi_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}}({PROD\_NEW})\bowtie\allowbreak\pi_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}}({PROD\_NEW})
.
However, attribute

CATEGORY

is not summarizable from
\mathrm{X}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}}\}
because the natural join between

\pi_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}}}({PROD\_NEW})
and
\pi_{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}}({PROD\_NEW})
eliminates products ’p_03’ and ’p_04’.

.

To efficiently check summarizability,
(
Hurtado
et al. 2005
)
proposes to transform the summarizability problem into the problem of verifying the satisfaction of a set of dimension constraints by some dimension
{D}
. Let
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
be a dimension attribute of a dimension
{D}

and
\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i+1},...,\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i+n}\rangle
denote a path in
{D}
such that
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{k}\preccurlyeq\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{k+1}
,
i\leq k<i+n
.
Then, the following dimension constraints can be defined on
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{k}
and
{D}
:

(1)

D\models\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i+1},...,\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}\rangle
means that for every attribute value
v
of
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
,
there exists a tuple
t
in
{D}
such that
t.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}=v
and
t.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i+1}
, …,
t.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
are non-null (all values of
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
roll-up to a value
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
through a value of
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i+1}
…).
We shall say that
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}

rolls up to

\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
.

(2)

D\models\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i},...,\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}=k\rangle
means that for every attribute value
v
of
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}
,
there exists a tuple
t
in
{D}
such that
t.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i}=v
and
t.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
is not null if and only if
t.\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}=k
.

Constraints can then be composed using the usual Boolean logical connectives. Now, assume that a set of constraints are specified on the schema of
{D}
.
To determine if an attribute

B

is summarizable from
\mathrm{X}=\{A_{1},...,A_{n}\}
in
{D}
, one must determine if, for each bottom level attribute
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{\bot}
of
{D}
, the following constraint is satisfied :

(7)

\displaystyle D\models\langle A_{\bot},...,B\rangle\implies(\langle A_{\bot},...,A_{1},...,B\rangle\oplus...\oplus(\langle A_{\bot},...,A_{n},...,B\rangle)

where
\oplus
denotes an exclusive disjunction (XOR).
Intuitively, if all values of
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{\bot}
roll-up to a value of

B

then all these values either roll-up through values of
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{1}
or (exclusive) through values of
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{2}
… or through values of
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{n}
.
We use the following examples to illustrate the use of constraints to determine summarizability.

Example 0.

Suppose that in dimension
{PROD\_NEW}
,

VIDEO_RES

has now

SUBCATEGORY

as parent in the hierarchy type (
Figure

13
).
Then the two constraints (a) and (b) shown in
Figure

13
are expressed on
{PROD}
. Note that the disjunction constraint (a) is more expressive than the context dependency of
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
because it is not restricted to a single value.

(a)

Hierarchy type of
{PROD\_NEW}

Rules

(a)

\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}=
’TV’
\rangle

\oplus

\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}=
’Video projector’
\rangle

(b)

\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}\rangle
, for all other edges (
\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{i},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{A}}_{j}
)

Semantics

(a)

A value of

PROD_SKU

rolls up to

VIDEO_RES

and

SUBCATEGORY

only for the ’Video Projector’ and ’TV’ values of

SUBCATEGORY

(b)

All other attributes directly roll up to their parent attribute

(b)

Constraints on
{PROD\_NEW}

Figure 13
.
The dimension schema of
{PROD\_NEW}

The attribute

CATEGORY

is summarizable from
\mathrm{X}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}\}
because the following constraint is satisfied (all products roll up to a category through some subcategory):

(8)

\displaystyle{PROD}\models\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\ldots,\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}\rangle\Rightarrow\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}\rangle

Using the constraints in (b), we can compose
\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}\rangle
and
\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}\rangle
to yield the final constraint. Thus, if the table
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}
is first aggregated with a grouping set
\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}\,\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}\}
, then a query that further aggregates this result with a grouping set
\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}\}
is correct.

However, attribute

SUBCATEGORY

is not summarizable from
\mathrm{X}=\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}}\}
because the following constraint cannot be satisfied:

(9)

\displaystyle{PROD}\models\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}\rangle\Rightarrow\langle\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{PROD\_SKU}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}}\rangle

Thus, if table
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}
is first aggregated with a grouping set
\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{VIDEO\_RES}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}\}
, then a query
\mathpzc{Q}\,
that further aggregates this result with a grouping set
\{\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{SUBCATEGORY}},\mathrm{\scalebox{0.8}{CATEGORY}}\}
is incorrect.
If the tuples of
\mathrm{{PROD\_NEW\_SALES}}
with

SUBCATEGORY

attribute values ’TV’ or ’Video projector’ are filtered out then the summarizability constraint can be satisfied and previous query
\mathpzc{Q}\,
will be correct.

It is clear that the data model and constraints proposed by
(
Hurtado
et al. 2005
)
subsume the data model with context dependencies of
(
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
)
.
We already showed that our summarizability conditions are more expressive than context dependencies by considering
null
values as regular values for aggregation. The same arguments apply to dimension constraints.
In addition,
(
Hurtado
et al. 2005
)
has the following limitations with respect to our work. First,
any non-null value of a mandatory attribute must map to a single parent value in the hierarchy. This discards the use of dimension tables like
{SALESORG}
in
Table

8(b)
. Second, measure attributes cannot depend on a subset of the dimensions of a fact table. This discards the use of fact tables resulting from interactive user queries like the result of the left-merge of
\mathrm{{T2}}
with
\mathrm{{DEM^{\prime}}}
in
Table

6(b)
, presented in the Introduction section. Finally, unlike other works, the notion of applicability of an aggregation function to an attribute is not covered.

6.
Conclusions

In this article, we introduce a new framework for controlling the correctness of aggregation operations during sessions of interactive analytic queries.
Our framework adopts an
attribute-centric view
, whereby
aggregable properties
of attributes are used to describe and control the interaction between measures, dimensions and aggregation functions.
As a first advantage, aggregable properties enable the designers of analytic tables to describe the wide variety of semantic properties of measure attributes with respect to their dimensions defined in previous work
(
Kimball and Ross 2013
;
Horner
et al. 2004
;
Niemi et al. 2014
;
Stevens 1946
;
Pedersen
et al. 2001
;
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
)
. Another advantage of aggregable properties is their ability to guarantee that aggregate queries over some attributes can only be expressed if these attributes are summarizable.
We provide two definitions of summarizable attributes. Our first definition covers the case when an aggregate query is defined over the result of another aggregate query; it subsumes the definitions of previous work on summarizability
(
Lenz and Shoshani 1997
;
Pedersen
et al. 2001
;
Lehner
et al. 1998
;
Lechtenbörger and Vossen 2003
;
Hurtado
et al. 2005
)
. Our second definition introduces the new notion of
G-summarizability
which applies in the case of an aggregate query defined over the result of an arbitrary analytic queries. The two definitions are complementary. Our main technical results are the definition of
propagation
rules that automatically compute the aggregable properties of attributes in the result of an analytic query knowing the aggregable properties of the attributes in the operand tables of the query. We progressively refine our propagation rules to handle the semantic properties of measures, and the summarizability and G-summarizability properties of attributes.

There is a number of perspectives for future work.
First, aggregable properties could be extended to handle other correctness issues of aggregate queries. Currently, we rely on literal functional dependencies (LFD) for analyzing the summarizability properties of query results.
Simpson’s paradox
(
Wagner 1982
)
is an example of incorrect causal interpretation of aggregated attribute values where a statistical observation like ratio or bias on the measures from several partitions might disappear or be inverted on the aggregated measures over these partitions.
Aggregable properties could be extended to guard against this kind of statistical errors by exploiting existing causal dependencies between table attributes
(
Pearl
et al. 2016
)
(representing features) in addition to LFD.

Another direction of research is the "explainability" of aggregated values in an analytic table resulting from an interactive data analysis session. Aggregable properties provide explanations for the decision to forbid an incorrect aggregate query on a table. They also help the user to backtrack in her session to find an intermediate result over which a desired aggregation can be expressed. However, as shown in
Example

4.19
, a priori non-summarizable aggregate queries could be accepted provided that adequate annotations are added to the result table so that aggregated values can be properly interpreted. Generating such minimal annotations and propagating them is still open.

Finally, although a large part of our data model has already been prototyped in SAP HANA
(
Rutian 2020
)
, a significant effort is needed to integrate our framework into self-service data preparation tools and analytic database systems.

Another possible direction is the implementation of our framework as an independent software component,
e.g
.
Python library, which could be part of data preparation pipelines for Deep Learning applications (Python notebooks).
